response:
  reply: "# Harbor 2 matter dossier\n\n## Current position\n\nThe Harbor Pay migration
    needs legal and compliance review before the planned Nov 15 close. This is the
    accepted working view. It is not a recorded decision.\n\nMosaic plans an asset
    purchase followed by a day-one migration of 240,000 consumer users and 12,000
    business accounts into one Mosaic account and balance. Full product access is
    planned.\n\nThe main unresolved obstacle is whether Mosaic can lawfully provide
    the post-close services in each state. Mosaic should not assume that Harbor’s
    money transmitter licenses move with the purchased assets.\n\nOther material gaps
    are:\n\n1. Mosaic has not confirmed that its anti-money-laundering and customer
    identification program permits reliance on Harbor’s prior know-your-customer results.\n2.
    Underlying identity documents are missing for users onboarded before 2023.\n3.
    Harbor’s approximately 40 open alerts and recent suspicious activity report history
    remain unread.\n4. Harbor’s monitoring service ends at close. The replacement
    feed has not been shown to work without a gap.\n5. The structure and terms of
    the planned $50 welcome bonus are unknown.\n\nThe full day-one migration remains
    the planning baseline. The saved working view supports state launch holds, account
    restrictions, or delayed product access if the relevant conditions are not resolved.
    No decisions are recorded.\n\n### Assumptions used\n\n- Harbor is a licensed money
    transmitter subject to applicable state and federal anti-money-laundering requirements.
    This remains an open assumption and affects all licensing, customer identification,
    alert, and monitoring conclusions.\n- Mosaic will become the operating provider
    immediately after close. This affects whether Harbor could remain the licensed
    provider during a transition.\n- The analysis does **not** assume that Harbor’s
    licenses transfer, that Harbor’s prior identity checks are sufficient, or that
    the monitoring transition will work without a gap.\n- The earlier assumption that
    the acquisition structure was unknown is replaced by the reported fact that the
    deal is an asset purchase.\n- Mosaic’s reported 41-state license footprint is
    treated as a reported fact, not an assumption.\n\n### Record conflicts\n\n- Mosaic
    reportedly has licenses in 41 states, while the combined footprint is 44 states.
    `44 − 41 = 3`, not four. The same record says the transaction opens four new markets.
    The four stated markets therefore do not reconcile with the state totals. This
    affects which jurisdictions need new authority or launch restrictions. The license
    lists and the meaning of “market” are needed to resolve the conflict.\n- Matter
    metadata gives a target date of `2026-09-11`. The reported acquisition close is
    “Nov 15,” approximately ten weeks after the Sept. 9 intake. The saved timing-test
    hypothetical uses November 15, 2026, but a hypothetical date does not establish
    the actual closing year. The operative closing date and year must be confirmed.\n\n##
    Decision question\n\nDoes any part of the Harbor Pay acquisition migration need
    legal review before the Nov 15 close?\n\n## Issues\n\n<!-- issue:ISS-c9d7c39f63-94e42e2b38090045
    -->\n### Money transmission licensing: whether Mosaic Relay can operate under
    the combined 44-state footprint and whether Harbor's MTLs transfer/survive the
    acquisition\n\n**Current answer**\n\nMosaic should not treat Harbor’s money transmitter
    licenses as assets that automatically move to Mosaic in the asset purchase.\n\nBefore
    Mosaic starts regulated services in a state, it needs documented authority for
    its complete post-close activity. The acquisition may close separately from the
    service launch. Where authority is not established, the saved working position
    is to hold regulated functions or use a supported interim structure.\n\n**Rule
    and support**\n\nThe saved research concludes that the answer must be determined
    state by state. The review must cover Mosaic’s operating authority, transaction
    notices and approvals, interim operation, outstanding obligations, and Harbor’s
    license surrender.\n\nFour sources were retrieved. No relevant passages were read
    or verified. The retrieved materials therefore do not establish an operative state
    rule:\n\n- [Research packet](03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/research/RES-20260911-0a5f07.md)\n-
    [CSBS Money Transmission Modernization Act — retrieved; relevant passage not reviewed](03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/research/sources/SRC-37ee676da5b354ba8538-157583424427.md)\n-
    [New York Department of Financial Services licensing page — retrieved; relevant
    passage not reviewed](03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/research/sources/SRC-71b20ecf65825d6522c4-fa80c95c5393.md)\n\nThe
    model act is not proof of the law in any specific state.\n\n**Application**\n\nHarbor
    reportedly holds licenses in 38 states. Mosaic reportedly holds licenses in 41
    states. The combined footprint is reported as 44 states. Mosaic currently plans
    to use or assume Harbor’s licenses for the new markets.\n\nThe unified Mosaic
    account, combined balance, and access to Mosaic products suggest that Mosaic may
    become the actual provider. If so, keeping Harbor’s entity or licenses active
    might not give Mosaic authority.\n\nHarbor could remain the licensed provider
    during a transition only if the customer contracts, funds flow, operating control,
    licenses, bonds, records, and applicable state requirements support that result.
    The structure must match the actual service.\n\nA partner bank’s custody of funds
    would not, by itself, establish Mosaic’s authority. The answer depends on who
    contracts with users, controls transfers, owes repayment, maintains balances,
    handles errors, and carries the regulated obligations.\n\n**Next step**\n\nResolve
    the footprint conflict and identify every Harbor-only jurisdiction. Prepare a
    state matrix that covers:\n\n- Mosaic’s authority for each post-close activity;\n-
    notices, filings, approvals, and deadlines;\n- authority to operate while an application
    is pending;\n- customer agreement and outstanding obligation transfers;\n- bank
    custody and control;\n- Harbor’s run-off duties; and\n- license surrender.\n\nDo
    not activate regulated functions in a jurisdiction without documented authority.\n\n<details>\n<summary>Saved
    research and detailed prior analysis</summary>\n\nThe saved closing rule is:\n\n>
    **No confirmed authority means no regulated launch by Mosaic in that state.**\n\nA
    launch could proceed only if Mosaic has its own license, the state approves the
    required change, the regulator supports an interim structure, Harbor remains the
    real provider under a permitted transition, or regulated functions are blocked.\n\nThe
    state review must not be limited to a rule named “license transfer.” It should
    also examine:\n\n- sale of substantially all assets;\n- change of control;\n-
    change in business plan or licensed activity;\n- assignment of payment obligations;\n-
    changes to responsible persons, locations, or agents;\n- customer agreement assignment;\n-
    movement of stored balances or customer funds; and\n- license surrender or termination.\n\nAn
    asset purchase might fall outside a technical change-of-control rule because ownership
    of Harbor does not change. That would not resolve whether Harbor needs notice
    or surrender approval or whether Mosaic needs its own authority.\n\nA Harbor transition
    structure would need to show that Harbor remains responsible to customers, is
    named in the customer terms, retains the required control, keeps its licenses
    and financial coverage active, handles reports and complaints, and maintains required
    records. Mosaic’s role must be permitted. Relevant regulators and the partner
    bank may also need to accept the structure.\n\nThe planned single Mosaic account,
    combined balance, and full Mosaic product access make it harder to show that Harbor
    remains the actual provider.\n\nFor any bank-held funds structure, the saved analysis
    identifies these questions:\n\n1. Who receives the customer’s transfer instruction?\n2.
    Who accepts the obligation to transmit or return funds?\n3. In whose name is the
    bank account held?\n4. Who controls withdrawals?\n5. Who owns the ledger and resolves
    errors?\n6. Who bears losses from failed transfers?\n7. Who owes the customer
    a refund?\n8. Which entity’s license, bond, and permissible investments cover
    the obligation?\n9. Does the bank agreement permit assignment or a program-manager
    change?\n10. What happens to funds if the Harbor, Mosaic, or bank relationship
    ends?\n\nThe review should also cover segregation, reconciliation, shortfalls,
    bank setoff rights, liens, pending transfers, returns, insolvency treatment, customer
    disclosures, and the accuracy of any deposit-insurance statement.\n\nThe saved
    recommendation separates acquisition closing from regulated-service launch. Harbor’s
    licenses and bonds should not be released until applicable requirements are satisfied
    and outstanding obligations are completed, assigned, or placed into an approved
    run-off structure.\n\nResearch status: four sources retrieved; no relevant passages
    read or verified.\n\n</details>\n\n<!-- retained-issue:ISS-c9d7c39f63-94e42e2b38090045:start
    -->\n<details>\n<summary>Saved research and prior analysis</summary>\n\nDo not
    rely on transferability without an applicable state rule, approval, or written
    regulator position.\n\nThe answer must be determined state by state and must cover
    more than technical change-of-control rules.\n\nThis path is possible only if
    Harbor remains the real provider and the relevant states permit the arrangement.
    The unified Mosaic account design may conflict with that position.\n\nThe result
    depends on the contracts and actual funds flow. Custody alone should not be treated
    as a licensing exemption or transfer mechanism.\n\nResearch saved. 4 sources retrieved;
    0 with passages read.\n\nNext step: Identify the four Harbor-only states and complete
    one matrix covering license authority, transaction filings, interim operation,
    bank custody, outstanding obligations, and license surrender.\n\n<details>\n<summary>Detailed
    analysis</summary>\n\n**Practical answer**\n\n**Do not treat Harbor’s money transmitter
    licenses as assets that move to Mosaic in the asset purchase.** Use this closing
    rule:\n\n> **No confirmed authority means no regulated launch by Mosaic in that
    state.**\n\nThe asset purchase can still close. However, Mosaic should not activate
    regulated services in the four Harbor-only states unless one of these conditions
    is met:\n\n1. Mosaic obtains its own license.\n2. The state approves a transfer
    or other license change.\n3. The regulator confirms a lawful interim structure.\n4.
    Harbor remains the real licensed provider under a valid transition plan.\n5. Mosaic
    blocks regulated functions in that state.\n\nThe planned unified Mosaic account
    is important. It suggests that Mosaic, not Harbor, will become the service provider.
    If that is correct, keeping Harbor’s legal entity and licenses alive might not
    give Mosaic authority to operate.\n\n**No verified operative authority retrieved.**
    The collection found the CSBS Model Money Transmission Modernization Act and a
    New York regulator licensing page. However, no exact provision about an asset
    sale, license transfer, interim operation, or custody was available for review.
    Also, a model act is not proof of the law adopted in a specific state. The state-specific
    conclusions below remain conditional.\n\n**Work needed before launch**\n\n| Work
    | Why | Proposed owner | Needed by | Evidence to proceed | Fallback |\n|---|---|---|---|---|---|\n|
    Identify the four Harbor-only states | These states have the clearest authority
    gap | Licensing team | Now | Confirmed license list and covered activities | Block
    regulated functions in those states |\n| Build a matrix for every affected state
    | Transfer, notice, approval, and surrender rules can differ | State regulatory
    counsel | Before the closing conditions are final | Applicable rule, filing deadline,
    regulator response, and license status | Delay migration in the affected state
    |\n| Confirm the post-close service provider | The licensed entity must match
    the actual operating model | Product and deal counsel | Before customer notices
    and account design are final | Contracts, user interface, funds-flow diagram,
    and operating roles | Keep Harbor as the actual provider where permitted |\n|
    Review notices and approvals | An asset sale can create separate seller and buyer
    requirements | Licensing team | Before each state deadline | Filing receipts and
    required approvals or non-objections | Make approval a state launch condition
    |\n| Confirm interim operating authority | A pending application should not be
    assumed to permit operation | State regulatory counsel | Before close | Applicable
    rule or written regulator confirmation | Keep Harbor customers segregated or restrict
    transactions |\n| Review partner-bank arrangements | Bank custody does not, by
    itself, establish Mosaic’s licensing position | Payments counsel and bank relationship
    owner | Before balances move | Bank agreements, account titles, control rights,
    reconciliation design, and bank consent | Keep funds and obligations in the existing
    structure |\n| Prepare license run-off and surrender plans | Harbor may retain
    duties for balances, claims, records, and reports | Harbor compliance and deal
    counsel | Before surrender or operational shutdown | Regulator-approved plan and
    continued bonds and financial coverage | Keep Harbor licensed and operational
    longer |\n\n**State notice and approval**\n\nDo not search only for a rule called
    “license transfer.” Review each state for:\n\n- sale of substantially all assets;\n-
    change of control;\n- change in business plan;\n- change in licensed activities;\n-
    assignment of outstanding payment obligations;\n- change in responsible persons,
    locations, or agents;\n- license surrender or termination;\n- customer agreement
    assignment; and\n- movement of stored balances or customer funds.\n\nAn asset
    purchase might fall outside a technical “change of control” rule because Harbor’s
    ownership does not change. That does not resolve the issue. Harbor might still
    need a notice or surrender approval. Mosaic might separately need authority before
    it starts the regulated activity.\n\nThe state matrix should distinguish:\n\n-
    notice only;\n- approval before closing;\n- approval before customer migration;\n-
    new license required;\n- operation permitted while an application is pending;\n-
    regulator-approved transition available; and\n- no supported interim path.\n\n**Interim
    operation**\n\nHarbor could remain the licensed provider for a transition period.
    However, the structure must be real, not only contractual language.\n\nA supportable
    structure would normally need to show that:\n\n- Harbor remains responsible to
    customers.\n- Customer terms name Harbor as the provider.\n- Harbor controls the
    regulated activity as required.\n- Harbor’s licenses, bonds, and financial coverage
    remain active.\n- Customer funds and outstanding obligations remain covered.\n-
    Harbor can complete reports, refunds, complaints, and examinations.\n- Required
    records remain available.\n- Mosaic’s operational role is permitted.\n- The partner
    bank accepts the structure.\n- Each relevant regulator permits or accepts the
    structure.\n\nThe present plan creates tension with this approach. A single Mosaic
    account, one combined balance, and full access to Mosaic products make it harder
    to show that Harbor remains the true provider.\n\n**Partner-bank custody**\n\nA
    partner bank account is not automatically a substitute for a money transmitter
    license. The key questions concern the complete activity and obligation flow.\n\nFor
    each customer balance and transfer, document:\n\n1. Who receives the customer’s
    instruction?\n2. Who accepts the obligation to transmit or return the funds?\n3.
    In whose name is the bank account held?\n4. Is it a custodial or “for benefit
    of” account?\n5. Who can direct withdrawals?\n6. Who owns the ledger and resolves
    errors?\n7. Who bears loss if a transfer fails?\n8. Which entity owes the customer
    a refund?\n9. Which entity’s license, bond, and permissible investments cover
    the obligation?\n10. Does the bank agreement permit assignment, migration, or
    a change in the program manager?\n11. Is bank consent required before closing
    or migration?\n12. What happens to funds if Harbor, Mosaic, or the bank relationship
    ends?\n\nA bank-led model might provide a different licensing analysis if the
    bank is the actual provider and Mosaic performs only permitted services. A bank
    account that merely holds funds for Mosaic or Harbor is different. The legal documents,
    customer terms, control rights, and actual operations must all support the claimed
    model.\n\nAlso review:\n\n- account segregation;\n- daily reconciliation;\n- shortfalls
    and reserve funding;\n- bank setoff rights;\n- liens and security interests;\n-
    continuity if the account is frozen;\n- treatment of pending transfers;\n- return
    and chargeback duties;\n- insolvency treatment;\n- customer disclosures; and\n-
    whether any deposit-insurance statement is accurate for the actual account structure.\n\n**Recommended
    closing position**\n\nKeep the acquisition closing separate from the regulated-service
    launch.\n\nFor the four Harbor-only states, use a state launch hold unless Mosaic
    has documented authority. For overlapping states, confirm that Mosaic’s existing
    licenses cover the complete post-close product set and funds flow.\n\nDo not surrender
    Harbor’s licenses or release its bonds until the applicable regulators permit
    it and all outstanding obligations have been assigned, completed, or placed into
    an approved run-off structure.\n\nThis investigation does not change the current
    recommendation. It adds one material point: **partner-bank custody is part of
    the licensing analysis, but custody alone does not cure an authority gap.**\n\n</details>\n\nResearch
    history: [Saved research 1](03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/research/RES-20260911-0a5f07.md)\n\n</details>\n<!--
    retained-issue:ISS-c9d7c39f63-94e42e2b38090045:end -->\n\n<!-- issue:ISS-c9d7c39f63-ca95e529feeb1045
    -->\n### BSA/AML and KYC: whether relying on Harbor's prior KYC (with missing
    underlying documents for pre-2023 users) satisfies Mosaic Relay's independent
    CIP/AML obligations\n\n**Current answer**\n\nThe saved record does not support
    a full day-one migration with no further identity review.\n\nMosaic has pass/fail
    results for all users, but it lacks underlying documents for users onboarded before
    2023. Compliance has not confirmed whether Mosaic’s own anti-money-laundering
    and customer identification program permits reliance on Harbor’s work.\n\nThe
    missing documents establish a record gap. They do not, by themselves, prove that
    Harbor’s checks were invalid or that every affected user must be verified again.\n\n**Rule
    and support**\n\nThis issue has not been researched. No saved authority establishes
    the conditions under which Mosaic may rely on Harbor’s prior checks.\n\nThe applicable
    duty depends on Mosaic’s legal role, its written program, the rules that apply
    to that role, the type of account, and the records Harbor can lawfully provide.
    An acquisition does not itself show that a prior verification result satisfies
    Mosaic’s program.\n\n**Application**\n\nThe plan covers 240,000 consumer users
    and 12,000 business accounts. It provides one Mosaic account and broad Mosaic
    product access without re-verification.\n\nThe reported pass/fail results may
    help, but the record does not show:\n\n- what Harbor checked;\n- which data and
    methods were used;\n- whether business owners and controllers were checked;\n-
    whether required records can be reproduced;\n- whether Mosaic’s risk model accepts
    Harbor’s process; or\n- whether later events require updated information.\n\nAccess
    to sensitive identity records must follow applicable privacy, security, and contractual
    limits. Permitted diligence is not an instruction to disclose or transfer restricted
    material.\n\n**Next step**\n\nCompliance should compare Harbor’s process and available
    evidence with Mosaic’s written program. It should identify affected users and
    define a documented response, such as acceptance of sufficient records, targeted
    remediation, account restrictions, or re-verification.\n\nThis conclusion depends
    on Mosaic’s regulated role and program requirements, which remain unconfirmed.\n\n<!--
    retained-issue:ISS-c9d7c39f63-ca95e529feeb1045:start -->\nInitial analysis — no
    completed research is saved for this issue.\n<!-- retained-issue:ISS-c9d7c39f63-ca95e529feeb1045:end
    -->\n\n<!-- issue:ISS-c9d7c39f63-69af3f282d21ef38 -->\n### SAR and alert handling:
    obligations to review, file, and manage Harbor's ~40 open alerts and SARs, and
    successor liability\n\n**Current answer**\n\nThe approximately 40 open alerts
    and recent suspicious activity report history need controlled review before Mosaic
    gives migrated users full product access.\n\nThe current record does not establish
    that Mosaic inherits every Harbor duty or liability. It also does not support
    ignoring unresolved cases merely because the deal is an asset purchase.\n\n**Rule
    and support**\n\nThis issue has not been researched. No saved authority establishes
    which entity must finish Harbor’s alerts, make filing decisions, retain related
    records, or respond after close.\n\nThe answer depends on the regulated actors,
    transaction documents, retained and assumed obligations, timing of suspicious
    conduct, filing deadlines, and applicable confidentiality rules.\n\nSuspicious
    activity report information requires controlled access. The dossier does not direct
    Harbor to disclose protected material broadly. Counsel must identify what Mosaic
    may review during diligence and what records may be retained or transferred.\n\n**Application**\n\nDiligence
    identified approximately 40 open alerts and “a handful” of suspicious activity
    reports from the last year. None have been reviewed.\n\nThe unread cases could
    show ordinary unresolved reviews, filing deadlines, customer risk, control failures,
    or conduct that affects transaction terms. The present record cannot distinguish
    among those possibilities.\n\nThe reported asset purchase clarifies the deal structure.
    It does not answer how the purchase agreement allocates open investigations, filing
    work, records, cooperation, or losses.\n\n**Next step**\n\nConduct a controlled
    review of the alert inventory and available suspicious activity history. Record
    each case’s age, status, relevant entity, deadline, required access controls,
    proposed disposition, and post-close owner.\n\nUse the results to decide whether
    specific accounts require restrictions, whether the purchase agreement needs added
    protections, and whether any issue affects closing.\n\n<!-- retained-issue:ISS-c9d7c39f63-69af3f282d21ef38:start
    -->\nInitial analysis — no completed research is saved for this issue.\n<!-- retained-issue:ISS-c9d7c39f63-69af3f282d21ef38:end
    -->\n\n<!-- issue:ISS-c9d7c39f63-4825f0af7ec75542 -->\n### Monitoring vendor transition:
    continuity of transaction monitoring when the SaaS contract terminates at close\n\n**Current
    answer**\n\nMosaic should not rely on an untested day-one feed change. Monitoring
    coverage should be validated before migrated users receive unrestricted access.\n\nThe
    saved facts establish a continuity risk, not an actual monitoring failure.\n\n**Rule
    and support**\n\nThis issue has not been researched. No saved authority defines
    the required testing or transition control for these entities.\n\nThe relevant
    compliance question is whether the responsible regulated actor can monitor covered
    activity continuously and retain the information needed to investigate and report
    suspicious conduct.\n\n**Application**\n\nHarbor’s third-party monitoring contract
    ends at close. The plan is to redirect Harbor’s transaction feed to Mosaic’s engine
    on day one.\n\nThe record does not show completed mapping, historical-data access,
    alert calibration, duplicate handling, reconciliation, failure recovery, or end-to-end
    testing. A bad feed or incorrect rule mapping could leave transactions unreviewed
    even if the technical connection appears active.\n\nThe approximately 40 open
    alerts increase the need for a clear transition. Open cases must not be lost when
    the old service ends.\n\n**Next step**\n\nComplete and retain evidence of an end-to-end
    test before unrestricted migration. The evidence should cover data completeness,
    field mapping, alert generation, reconciliation, historical access, open-case
    migration, failure recovery, and accountable operating ownership.\n\nIf validation
    is incomplete, use a supported overlap, restrict affected activity, or delay full
    access.\n\n<!-- retained-issue:ISS-c9d7c39f63-4825f0af7ec75542:start -->\nInitial
    analysis — no completed research is saved for this issue.\n<!-- retained-issue:ISS-c9d7c39f63-4825f0af7ec75542:end
    -->\n\n<!-- issue:ISS-c9d7c39f63-72810d0a43d3ce66 -->\n### Marketing compliance:
    the $50 welcome bonus offer and any state money transmission or consumer protection
    implications\n\n**Current answer**\n\nThe $50 welcome bonus needs review before
    launch, but the saved record does not support a specific legal conclusion because
    the offer terms are unknown.\n\nDo not assume that the bonus is a deposit, interest
    payment, or prohibited promotion. Its treatment depends on how the offer works
    and how Mosaic describes it.\n\n**Rule and support**\n\nThis issue has not been
    researched. No saved authority addresses this offer.\n\nRelevant questions may
    include whether the offer and disclosures are clear, whether stated conditions
    match actual operation, how the bonus is funded and credited, which users and
    jurisdictions qualify, and whether the promoted transfer service is authorized
    where offered.\n\n**Application**\n\nMarketing wants to launch the offer on close+1.
    The record states only that a user would receive $50 on a first transfer.\n\nIt
    does not state:\n\n- eligibility rules;\n- minimum transfer amount;\n- funding
    source;\n- when the bonus becomes available;\n- withdrawal or reversal conditions;\n-
    exclusions;\n- expiration;\n- state coverage;\n- tax treatment; or\n- the wording
    of the advertisement and customer terms.\n\nThe campaign could also promote activity
    in a jurisdiction where Mosaic’s launch authority is still unresolved.\n\n**Next
    step**\n\nObtain the final offer mechanics, customer terms, advertisements, state
    coverage, funds flow, and product-launch dependencies. Review them before release.
    Exclude jurisdictions or functions that are not cleared for launch.\n\n<!-- retained-issue:ISS-c9d7c39f63-72810d0a43d3ce66:start
    -->\nInitial analysis — no completed research is saved for this issue.\n<!-- retained-issue:ISS-c9d7c39f63-72810d0a43d3ce66:end
    -->\n\n## Alternatives considered\n\n<!-- alternative:SCN-0f23edfc89ec6ecaf60a13e4
    -->\n### Bank-held funds timing test — **Hypothetical — not adopted**\n\n[Saved
    alternative](03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/scenarios/SCN-0f23edfc89ec6ecaf60a13e4.md)\n\n**Assumptions**\n\nFor
    this test only:\n\n- a bank holds the relevant funds;\n- Mosaic controls transfer
    instructions, subject to completion of the bank contract and operating setup;\n-
    migration is planned for November 15, 2026;\n- bank integration is ready on December
    3, 2026;\n- pre-2023 identity documents remain missing;\n- 40 alert cases remain
    pending; and\n- the current monitoring service ends at close.\n\n**How it helps**\n\nBank
    custody could change the funds-flow analysis and identify a possible operating
    structure. The saved material does not establish that custody removes Mosaic’s
    licensing duties.\n\n**How it hinders**\n\nOn the supplied test dates, the bank
    is ready 18 days after the proposed migration:\n\n`December 3, 2026 − November
    15, 2026 = 18 days`\n\nThe option therefore makes the November 15 hypothetical
    migration harder unless the contract and operating setup can be completed earlier.\n\n**Added
    risks**\n\nThe option creates a dependency on bank contracting, integration, consent,
    control rights, and operating readiness. Mosaic would still control transfer instructions
    under the hypothetical, so the bank’s custody alone does not decide which entity
    performs the regulated activity.\n\n**Issues it leaves unresolved**\n\nThe option
    does not resolve:\n\n- missing pre-2023 identity documents;\n- the 40 pending
    alerts;\n- suspicious activity handling;\n- monitoring ending at close;\n- which
    entity contracts with users;\n- who owes customers repayment; or\n- whether Mosaic
    has authority for the full service.\n\n**Next action**\n\nThe saved working note
    proposes that Mara Chen obtain a dated bank-readiness plan and confirm whether
    the bank can support November 15. It also proposes assigning responsibility for
    identity-record remediation, pending alerts, and replacement monitoring. This
    is proposed work, not an assigned action.\n\n<!-- alternative:SCN-406906c91ab0aba4313b54a6
    -->\n### Bank-held funds structure — **Hypothetical — not adopted**\n\n[Saved
    alternative](03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/scenarios/SCN-406906c91ab0aba4313b54a6.md)\n\n**Assumptions**\n\nFor
    this hypothetical only, a partner bank holds customer funds. The actual acquisition
    remains an asset purchase.\n\n**How it helps**\n\nThe structure could support
    a different legal analysis if the bank is the actual provider and Mosaic performs
    only permitted services. The saved record does not establish that operating model.\n\n**How
    it hinders**\n\nNo disadvantage is established from bank custody alone. However,
    a new bank structure would depend on agreed contracts, control rights, customer
    terms, and operating processes. If those items are not completed in time, the
    structure cannot support the planned migration.\n\n**Added risks**\n\nThe legal
    position remains uncertain if Mosaic controls transfers, maintains balances, owes
    repayment, or is presented to users as the provider. Bank custody alone does not
    cure an authority gap.\n\n**Issues it leaves unresolved**\n\nThe following facts
    remain unknown:\n\n- who contracts directly with users;\n- who controls transfer
    instructions and settlement;\n- who records balances and owes repayment;\n- how
    customer identification duties are allocated;\n- who performs monitoring and alert
    review;\n- who handles suspicious activity reports;\n- how privacy duties are
    allocated; and\n- who is responsible for marketing statements.\n\n**Next action**\n\nPrepare
    the proposed contracts, customer terms, and funds-flow diagram. Identify each
    entity’s actual role before using this structure as a basis for licensing or launch
    conclusions. No owner or due date is recorded.\n\n## Next actions\n\nAll open
    actions below are proposed unless stated otherwise. No legal or business decisions
    are recorded.\n\n1. **Review the open alerts and suspicious activity history under
    controlled access.**  \n   **Owner:** Not recorded.  \n   **Date:** No due date
    recorded; the saved view requires review before unrestricted migration.  \n   **Evidence
    needed:** Case inventory, age, status, deadlines, permitted-access record, proposed
    disposition, and post-close responsibility.\n\n2. **Resolve the footprint conflict
    and complete the state authority matrix.**  \n   **Proposed owners:** Licensing
    team and state regulatory counsel.  \n   **Date:** No actual due date recorded;
    required before affected state launches and relevant closing conditions are final.
    \ \n   **Evidence needed:** Harbor and Mosaic license lists, covered activities,
    applicable rules, filing receipts, approvals or non-objections, regulator responses,
    and run-off requirements.\n\n3. **Confirm whether Mosaic’s program permits reliance
    on Harbor’s prior identity checks.**  \n   **Proposed owner:** Compliance.  \n
    \  **Date:** No actual due date recorded; required before unrestricted migration.
    \ \n   **Evidence needed:** Mosaic’s written program, Harbor’s procedures, available
    verification records, affected-user population, gap analysis, and approved remediation
    plan.\n\n4. **Validate the monitoring transition end to end.**  \n   **Owner:**
    Not recorded.  \n   **Date:** No actual due date recorded; required before the
    current service ends at close.  \n   **Evidence needed:** Test results for data
    completeness, field mapping, alert generation, reconciliation, historical access,
    open-case transfer, and failure recovery.\n\n5. **Review the $50 welcome offer
    after its terms are complete.**  \n   **Owner:** Not recorded.  \n   **Date:**
    No actual due date recorded; review is needed before the planned close+1 launch.
    \ \n   **Evidence needed:** Final terms, advertisements, eligibility, state coverage,
    funds flow, restrictions, credit and reversal mechanics, and disclosures.\n\n6.
    **For the bank-held funds timing test, obtain a dated readiness plan.**  \n   **Proposed
    owner:** Mara Chen.  \n   **Date:** No due date is assigned. The hypothetical
    compares November 15, 2026 with December 3, 2026 readiness.  \n   **Evidence needed:**
    Bank contract schedule, integration plan, operating controls, approvals, dependencies,
    and confirmed support date.\n\nThe only recorded work item, **Orient to the request**,
    was assigned to the Lawyer and completed on September 9, 2026.\n\n## Current work
    product and supporting records\n\n- **Current work product:** No current draft
    or final work-product path is recorded.\n- [Original request](03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/request.md)\n-
    [Reported facts and assumptions](03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/facts.md)\n- [Money
    transmission research packet](03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/research/RES-20260911-0a5f07.md)\n-
    [Matter conversation](03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/conversations/CONV-20260909-5299cf.md)\n-
    [Prior dossier revision](03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/dossier-revisions/DOS-6e1b2d11381df87e0eab.md)\n-
    [Bank-held funds timing test](03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/scenarios/SCN-0f23edfc89ec6ecaf60a13e4.md)\n-
    [Bank-held funds structure](03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/scenarios/SCN-406906c91ab0aba4313b54a6.md)\n\n[Open
    saved dossier revision](03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/dossier-revisions/DOS-a339a10414a6329eff9f.md)\n\nDossier
    generated and saved."

  - source_id: MSG-20260909-7825a0
    source_label: 'Saved message: I''ve recorded the intake. Here''s my orientation
      to the request.


      **Summary of th…'
    source_class: saved_artifact
    path: 03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/conversations/CONV-20260909-5299cf.md
    available_excerpt: 'I''ve recorded the intake. Here''s my orientation to the request.


      **Summary of the request:** Dana (PM, Growth & Integration) asks whether the
      Harbor Pay acquisition migration needs legal review before the Nov 15 close
      (~10 weeks out). The plan moves 240k consumer users and 12k business accounts
      into Mosaic Relay''s app on day 1, with no re-verification of Harbor''s KYC,
      repointing Harbor''s monitoring feed to Mosaic''s engine, a $50 welcome bonus,
      and unread open alerts/SARs. Dana frames it as "really just a data migration."


      **My read:** This is not just a data migration. It raises at least five legal
      workstreams that need attention before close:


      - **Money transmission licensing** — the combined 44-state footprint and whether
      Harbor''s MTLs transfer or must be re-applied for in the four new markets.

      - **BSA/AML and KYC** — whether Mosaic can rely on Harbor''s prior KYC without
      re-verification, especially where pre-2023 underlying documents are missing.

      - **SARs and open alerts** — ~40 open alerts and a handful of SARs carry potential
      unresolved BSA/AML obligations and successor liability.

      - **Monitoring vendor transition** — continuity of transaction monitoring when
      Harbor''s SaaS contract terminates at close.

      - **Marketing compliance** — the $50 welcome bonus offer.


      The most decision-changing unknown is the **acquisition structure** (asset vs.
      stock vs. merger), because it determines whether Harbor''s licenses and regulatory
      obligations transfer to Mosaic or must be re-established. Please answer the
      four questions in the intake card — structure, KYC reliance, SAR/alerts status,
      and the new-market licensing plan — and I''ll build the issue map.'
    record_revision: 7c9bf9ec8a4c04a0e97d72c4dfaa269cca4316533618f000ed0084569e165bcc
    source_version: 7c9bf9ec8a4c04a0e97d72c4dfaa269cca4316533618f000ed0084569e165bcc
    support_state: supplied
    captured_record:
      message_id: MSG-20260909-7825a0
      content: 'I''ve recorded the intake. Here''s my orientation to the request.


        **Summary of the request:** Dana (PM, Growth & Integration) asks whether the
        Harbor Pay acquisition migration needs legal review before the Nov 15 close
        (~10 weeks out). The plan moves 240k consumer users and 12k business accounts
        into Mosaic Relay''s app on day 1, with no re-verification of Harbor''s KYC,
        repointing Harbor''s monitoring feed to Mosaic''s engine, a $50 welcome bonus,
        and unread open alerts/SARs. Dana frames it as "really just a data migration."


        **My read:** This is not just a data migration. It raises at least five legal
        workstreams that need attention before close:


        - **Money transmission licensing** — the combined 44-state footprint and whether
        Harbor''s MTLs transfer or must be re-applied for in the four new markets.

        - **BSA/AML and KYC** — whether Mosaic can rely on Harbor''s prior KYC without
        re-verification, especially where pre-2023 underlying documents are missing.

        - **SARs and open alerts** — ~40 open alerts and a handful of SARs carry potential
        unresolved BSA/AML obligations and successor liability.

        - **Monitoring vendor transition** — continuity of transaction monitoring
        when Harbor''s SaaS contract terminates at close.

        - **Marketing compliance** — the $50 welcome bonus offer.


        The most decision-changing unknown is the **acquisition structure** (asset
        vs. stock vs. merger), because it determines whether Harbor''s licenses and
        regulatory obligations transfer to Mosaic or must be re-established. Please
        answer the four questions in the intake card — structure, KYC reliance, SAR/alerts
        status, and the new-market licensing plan — and I''ll build the issue map.'
      role: assistant
      path: 03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/conversations/CONV-20260909-5299cf.md

    path: 03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/conversations/CONV-20260909-5299cf.md
    available_excerpt: '## Initial issue map


      - **Licensing:** In an asset purchase, Harbor’s licenses may not transfer to
      Mosaic. The four new markets need state-by-state clearance before launch.

      - **BSA/AML:** Mosaic’s policy on prior KYC is unknown. Missing records may
      require re-verification or access limits.

      - **Alerts and monitoring:** Review all open alerts and SAR history before close.
      Test monitoring continuity before migration.

      - **Marketing:** Review the $50 bonus when its terms are available.


      **No external authority retrieved.**


      ## Assumptions used


      - Mosaic will serve migrated users immediately after close.

      - Harbor’s licenses do not automatically transfer.

      - Harbor’s KYC results are not assumed sufficient.

      - The monitoring change can be tested before close.

      - The bonus structure remains unknown.


      Answer saved as a reported fact. The issue remains open.


      Question left open.'

  - source_id: MSG-20260909-32c7f4
    source_label: 'Saved message: **Issue** — The four workstreams are licensing,
      KYC/AML, alerts and monitoring, …'
    source_class: saved_artifact
    path: 03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/conversations/CONV-20260909-5299cf.md
    available_excerpt: '**Issue** — The four workstreams are licensing, KYC/AML, alerts
      and monitoring, and marketing.


      **Rule** — State licensing rules, Mosaic’s AML program, federal financial-crime
      duties, and consumer-protection rules may apply. **No external authority retrieved.**


      **Analysis**


      - **Licensing:** Asset purchases usually require state review of license transfer,
      assignment, or new applications. The four new markets may not be available at
      close.

      - **KYC/AML:** Mosaic must confirm whether its program permits reliance on Harbor’s
      checks. Missing records may require new verification or account limits.

      - **Alerts and monitoring:** Unresolved alerts need review. The monitoring transfer
      needs testing, audit records, and a plan that prevents coverage gaps.

      - **Marketing:** The $50 bonus needs clear eligibility, payment, expiry, tax,
      and advertising terms. State promotion rules may also apply.


      **Conclusion** — All four need legal or compliance review before launch. Licensing
      and unread alerts are the likely closing risks.'
    record_revision: 56492025c8493b9613b4adeda122209d408937bb34bbffa7710560ab2c4f3b9d
    source_version: 56492025c8493b9613b4adeda122209d408937bb34bbffa7710560ab2c4f3b9d
    support_state: supplied

      message_id: MSG-20260909-32c7f4

        - **KYC/AML:** Mosaic must confirm whether its program permits reliance on

  - source_id: MSG-20260910-2f255e
    source_label: 'Saved message: **Current approach:** Treat legal and compliance
      readiness as a pre-close gate. …'
    source_class: saved_artifact
    path: 03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/conversations/CONV-20260909-5299cf.md
    available_excerpt: '**Current approach:** Treat legal and compliance readiness
      as a pre-close gate. Do not assume Harbor’s licenses transfer. Consider a narrower
      launch if licensing, KYC records, alert review, or monitoring tests are incomplete.


      **Unresolved questions:** State approval in the four new markets; whether Mosaic’s
      AML/CIP program permits reliance on Harbor’s KYC; what the alerts and SAR history
      show; whether monitoring will work without a gap; and how the $50 bonus will
      operate.


      **Next step:** Obtain state-by-state licensing advice and review the alerts
      and SAR history. The available sources do not yet support a final state-specific
      conclusion.'

    available_excerpt: '**Issue** — A partner bank could hold customer funds, while

      controls transfers, owes balances, or acts directly for users. KYC, alert, SAR,
      monitoring, and marketing questions remain.


      **Conclusion** — Map the funds flow, customer relationship, and compliance duties
      before assessing this option. The current matter position stays unchanged.


      **No external authority retrieved for this hypothetical.**'

  - source_id: MSG-20260911-7d89cf
    source_label: 'Saved message: **Current approach**

      Mosaic becomes the operating provider after the asset purch…'
    source_class: saved_artifact
    path: 03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/conversations/CONV-20260909-5299cf.md
    available_excerpt: '**Current approach**

      Mosaic becomes the operating provider after the asset purchase.

      - **Benefit:** It needs fewer structural changes before migration.

      - **Risk:** Mosaic must confirm its authority in each state. Harbor’s licenses
      are not confirmed as transferable.

      - **Open work:** Confirm KYC reliance, review alerts and SAR history, and test
      monitoring before launch.

      **Bank-held funds structure**

      A partner bank holds customer funds. The other roles remain undefined.

      - **Possible benefit:** It could reduce Mosaic’s money-transmission exposure
      if the bank controls the accounts, transfers, settlement, balance records, and
      repayment.

      - **Risk:** Bank custody alone does not resolve Mosaic’s licensing status.

      - **Added work:** New bank contracts, user terms, funds flows, systems, and
      clear allocation of compliance duties.

      **Working view**

      Keep the current approach as the planning baseline. Consider a narrower launch
      if licensing, KYC, alert review, or monitoring work is incomplete.

      Use the bank structure only if a detailed operating model shows a clear regulatory
      benefit that is greater than the added work.

      **Next step**

      Prepare a funds-flow and responsibility map for the bank structure. Compare
      it with the state-by-state licensing determination.

      The bank structure remains hypothetical. Actual facts and the current direction
      are unchanged.

      **No external authority retrieved.** The licensing result remains unverified
      and can differ by state.

      authority does Mosaic need in each state under each structure?","why_it_matters":"The
      answer can prevent or limit launch in the four new markets.","kind":"applicability","part_keys":["current","bank"],"state":"open","priority":"decision_changing","next_action":"research","next_action_reason":"A
      state-by-state licensing determination is required."},{"key":"bank_control","question":"Who
      controls the customer relationship, accounts, transfers, settlement, balances,
      and repayment under the bank structure?","why_it_matters":"These activities
      can affect the legal characterization of Mosaic’s role.","kind":"characterization","part_keys":["bank"],"state":"open","priority":"decision_changing","next_action":"ask_business","next_action_reason":"The
      operating model and funds flow remain undefined."},{"key":"compliance","question":"How
      will KYC, monitoring, alerts, SARs, privacy, and marketing duties be allocated?","why_it_matters":"Bank
      custody does not automatically transfer these duties.","kind":"requirement","part_keys":["current","bank"],"state":"open","priority":"decision_changing","next_action":"ask_business","next_action_reason":"The
      contracts and responsibility map must assign each duty."}],"alternative_paths":[{"title":"Current
      approach","proposed_change":"Continue planning the day-one migration, subject
      to pre-close compliance gates.","benefit":"Avoids a late structural redesign.","tradeoff":"Leaves
      Mosaic with unresolved licensing and compliance risks.","remaining_condition":"Licensing
      authority, KYC reliance, alert handling, and monitoring readiness must be confirmed.","question_keys":["licensing","compliance"]},{"title":"Bank-held
      funds structure","proposed_change":"Use a partner bank to hold funds and define
      its contractual and operational control.","benefit":"Could reduce Mosaic’s money-transmission
      exposure if the full model supports that result.","tradeoff":"Adds contract,
      product, system, and operating work without automatically removing other duties.","remaining_condition":"The
      customer relationship, funds flow, control, repayment duty, and compliance allocation
      must be defined.","question_keys":["licensing","bank_control","compliance"]}],"integrated_answer":"The
      current approach remains the better planning baseline because the hypothetical
      bank structure has not shown a clear regulatory benefit and adds material implementation
      work. Both paths require state-specific licensing review and resolution of the
      existing financial-crime control gaps.","next_step":"Prepare the bank structure’s
      funds-flow and responsibility map, then compare it with state-specific licensing

      content: '**Current approach**

        Mosaic becomes the operating provider after the asset purchase.

        - **Benefit:** It needs fewer structural changes before migration.

        - **Risk:** Mosaic must confirm its authority in each state. Harbor’s licenses

        - **Open work:** Confirm KYC reliance, review alerts and SAR history, and

        **Bank-held funds structure**

        A partner bank holds customer funds. The other roles remain undefined.

        - **Possible benefit:** It could reduce Mosaic’s money-transmission exposure

  - source_id: MSG-20260911-8adc54
    source_label: 'Saved message: **Objective**


      Complete the Harbor acquisition and move users into Mosaic’s app …'
    source_class: saved_artifact
    path: 03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/conversations/CONV-20260909-5299cf.md
    available_excerpt: '**Objective**


      Complete the Harbor acquisition and move users into Mosaic’s app soon after
      close, without launching in a state where Mosaic lacks authority or creating
      a gap in financial-crime controls.


      ### Current approach


      Mosaic becomes the operating provider after the asset purchase and completes
      the migration on day one.


      **How it helps**


      - It uses the existing migration design.

      - It avoids a late change to contracts, systems, and customer communications.

      - It is more likely to support the planned timing if the open legal and compliance
      items are resolved.


      **How it hinders**


      - Harbor’s licenses might not transfer through an asset purchase. If Mosaic
      lacks authority in any of the four new markets, it may need to delay those markets.

      - Mosaic does not yet know whether its compliance program permits use of Harbor’s
      earlier identity checks without the underlying records.

      - Moving all users on day one increases the effect of any unresolved licensing,
      identity, alert, or monitoring problem.


      ### Bank-held funds structure


      A partner bank would hold customer funds. The bank’s other duties are not yet
      defined.


      **How it helps**


      - It could reduce Mosaic’s need for money-transmission licenses if the complete
      structure places the legally important activities with the bank.

      - It could provide a separate regulated party to hold funds and owe the related
      repayment duty.


      **How it hinders**


      - Bank custody alone does not establish that Mosaic can operate without licenses.
      Mosaic’s role in contracting with users, controlling transfer instructions,
      recording balances, and directing settlement remains important.

      - This structure needs new agreements, funds flows, user terms, system changes,
      and a clear division of duties.

      - Those changes could put the planned migration timing at risk.

      - The possible licensing benefit is not yet clear enough to show that it justifies
      the added work.


      ### Issues that remain under both


      1. **Open alerts and suspicious activity report history.** The existing alerts
      and filing history still need review. Changing who holds funds does not remove
      this diligence issue.


      2. **Identity verification records.** The parties must determine whether Mosaic
      can use Harbor’s earlier results and what to do when the underlying records
      are missing.


      3. **Monitoring continuity.** Mosaic must show that its monitoring system can
      receive and test Harbor’s transaction data without a gap when Harbor’s vendor
      contract ends.


      4. **State authority.** Each structure needs a state-by-state analysis. The
      question changes under the bank structure, but it does not disappear.


      5. **Allocation of duties.** Contracts and operating procedures must state who
      handles identity checks, monitoring, alerts, required filings, privacy duties,
      and customer communications.


      6. **Welcome bonus.** The offer terms and customer disclosures require review
      under either structure.


      **Working recommendation**


      Keep the current approach as the planning baseline, but use launch gates. Do
      not open a new market until Mosaic’s authority there is confirmed. Do not complete
      the full migration until the alert review, identity-record approach, and monitoring
      test have acceptable results.


      Use the bank structure only if a detailed funds-flow and responsibility map
      shows a material legal benefit that can be implemented before migration. A narrower
      launch is the practical fallback if the current approach cannot meet all gates.


      The bank structure remains hypothetical. It does not change the current direction
      or actual facts.


      **No external authority retrieved.** The licensing result is not verified and
      may differ by state.'
    record_revision: 63c900e986ede868cd9896eeb22b15f04b21c924bbba7a7d2ec1ab952cf73aab
    source_version: 63c900e986ede868cd9896eeb22b15f04b21c924bbba7a7d2ec1ab952cf73aab
    support_state: supplied

        a gap in financial-crime controls.


        ### Current approach

        the migration on day one.


        **How it helps**


        - It uses the existing migration design.

        - It avoids a late change to contracts, systems, and customer communications.

        - It is more likely to support the planned timing if the open legal and compliance

        **How it hinders**


        - Harbor’s licenses might not transfer through an asset purchase. If Mosaic
        lacks authority in any of the four new markets, it may need to delay those
        markets.

        - Mosaic does not yet know whether its compliance program permits use of Harbor’s
        earlier identity checks without the underlying records.

        - Moving all users on day one increases the effect of any unresolved licensing,
        identity, alert, or monitoring problem.


        ### Bank-held funds structure


        A partner bank would hold customer funds. The bank’s other duties are not
        yet defined.


        **How it helps**


        - It could reduce Mosaic’s need for money-transmission licenses if the complete
        structure places the legally important activities with the bank.

        - It could provide a separate regulated party to hold funds and owe the related
        repayment duty.


        **How it hinders**


        - Bank custody alone does not establish that Mosaic can operate without licenses.
        Mosaic’s role in contracting with users, controlling transfer instructions,
        recording balances, and directing settlement remains important.

        - This structure needs new agreements, funds flows, user terms, system changes,
        and a clear division of duties.

        - Those changes could put the planned migration timing at risk.

        - The possible licensing benefit is not yet clear enough to show that it justifies
        the added work.


        ### Issues that remain under both


        1. **Open alerts and suspicious activity report history.** The existing alerts
        and filing history still need review. Changing who holds funds does not remove
        this diligence issue.


        2. **Identity verification records.** The parties must determine whether Mosaic
        can use Harbor’s earlier results and what to do when the underlying records

        3. **Monitoring continuity.** Mosaic must show that its monitoring system
        can receive and test Harbor’s transaction data without a gap when Harbor’s
        vendor contract ends.


        4. **State authority.** Each structure needs a state-by-state analysis. The
        question changes under the bank structure, but it does not disappear.


        5. **Allocation of duties.** Contracts and operating procedures must state
        who handles identity checks, monitoring, alerts, required filings, privacy
        duties, and customer communications.


        6. **Welcome bonus.** The offer terms and customer disclosures require review
        under either structure.


        **Working recommendation**


        Keep the current approach as the planning baseline, but use launch gates.
        Do not open a new market until Mosaic’s authority there is confirmed. Do not
        complete the full migration until the alert review, identity-record approach,
        and monitoring test have acceptable results.

  - source_id: MSG-20260911-c9cbed
    source_label: 'Saved message: The **Current approach** better supports the planned
      close and day-one migration…'
    source_class: saved_artifact
    path: 03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/conversations/CONV-20260909-5299cf.md
    available_excerpt: 'The **Current approach** better supports the planned close
      and day-one migration. The **Bank-held funds structure** could reduce some state
      licensing risk, but only if the bank takes the legally important roles. It adds
      work and does not solve the existing financial-crime issues.


      **Objective**


      Complete the acquisition and migration on schedule. Avoid operating without
      required state authority. Prevent gaps in identity checks, transaction monitoring,
      and alert handling.


      ### Current approach


      Mosaic becomes the operating provider after the asset purchase.


      **How it helps**


      - It keeps the current product and migration design.

      - It avoids new bank agreements, user terms, and system connections.

      - It gives the best chance of meeting the day-one migration target.


      **How it hinders**


      - Mosaic plans to rely on Harbor’s licenses in four new markets. Their continued
      use after an asset purchase is not confirmed.

      - Mosaic would take the full effect of the unresolved identity, monitoring,
      and alert issues.

      - A single day-one migration increases the impact of a failure. One unresolved
      issue could affect all transferred users.


      This approach is practical only with launch gates. Mosaic should exclude a state
      or user group if the required review for that part is incomplete.


      ### Bank-held funds structure


      A partner bank holds customer funds. The remaining operating roles are not yet
      defined.


      **How it helps**


      - It could reduce Mosaic’s state money-transmission obligations.

      - That benefit is more likely if the bank contracts with users, controls transfers
      and settlement, records balances, and owes repayment to users.

      - It could support access to a market where Mosaic cannot obtain authority before
      launch.


      **How it hinders**


      - Holding funds is only one part of the legal analysis. Mosaic could still need
      state authority if it controls payment activity or has the main customer obligation.

      - The structure requires bank agreements, customer terms, funds-flow changes,
      technical integration, and operating procedures.

      - These changes could delay the migration.

      - The benefit cannot be measured until the bank’s role and Mosaic’s role are
      defined.


      This structure is therefore a possible licensing solution, not a complete compliance
      solution.


      ### Issues that remain under both


      1. **Open alerts and suspicious activity reports.** The parties must review
      the alerts and filing history. Bank custody does not resolve possible past problems
      or determine who must handle unresolved cases.


      2. **Identity records.** Mosaic must decide whether its compliance program permits
      use of Harbor’s earlier identity results. It also needs a plan for users whose
      underlying records are missing.


      3. **Monitoring continuity.** Harbor’s monitoring contract ends at close. The
      transaction feed must move to Mosaic’s system and be tested without a monitoring
      gap.


      4. **State-by-state authority.** The legal question changes under the bank structure,
      but it does not disappear. Each state must assess the activities performed by
      Mosaic, Harbor, and the bank.


      5. **Division of duties.** The operating documents must allocate identity checks,
      sanctions screening, monitoring, alert review, regulatory filings, privacy duties,
      and customer support.


      6. **Customer migration and data use.** Both approaches require review of the
      authority to transfer customer records, change terms, and give users access
      to Mosaic’s full product set.


      7. **Welcome bonus.** The offer terms, eligibility rules, disclosures, and funds
      flow need review under either structure.


      **Recommendation**


      Keep the **Current approach** as the planning baseline because it best supports
      the planned timing. Apply firm launch gates for state authority, identity records,
      alert review, and monitoring readiness.


      Continue the **Bank-held funds structure** only as a fallback or targeted market
      solution. Use it only if a detailed funds-flow and responsibility map shows
      a clear legal benefit that is greater than the added work.


      If the gates are not complete, the safest practical alternative is a narrower
      migration. Mosaic could delay the four new markets or affected user groups without
      delaying the whole acquisition.


      **No external authority retrieved.** State licensing and federal financial-crime
      conclusions remain provisional.'
    record_revision: 2f87221d2bafb3bfd7596db542f75834049c427088353ff61523b884c4946142
    source_version: 2f87221d2bafb3bfd7596db542f75834049c427088353ff61523b884c4946142

    source_class: saved_artifact
    path: 03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/conversations/CONV-20260909-5299cf.md
    available_excerpt: 'The **Current approach** best supports the planned day-one
      migration. The **Bank-held funds structure** could help in selected states,
      but its legal benefit is not yet clear. It also adds work that could delay the
      migration.


      **Objective**


      Complete the acquisition and migration on schedule. Avoid operating without
      required state authority. Prevent gaps in identity checks, transaction monitoring,
      and alert handling.


      ### Current approach


      Mosaic becomes the operating provider after the asset purchase.


      **How it helps**


      - It preserves the current product and migration plan.

      - It avoids new bank agreements, customer terms, and system links.

      - It gives Mosaic the better chance of meeting the day-one target.


      **How it hinders**


      - Mosaic plans to use or assume Harbor’s licenses in four new markets. Their
      continued use after an asset purchase is not confirmed.

      - Mosaic directly takes on the unresolved identity-record, monitoring, and alert
      issues.

      - A single large migration increases the effect of a failure.


      This approach needs firm launch gates. Mosaic should exclude a state or user
      group if the required review is not complete.


      ### Bank-held funds structure


      A partner bank holds customer funds. The other operating roles remain undefined.


      **How it helps**


      - It could reduce Mosaic’s state money-transmission obligations.

      - The benefit is more likely if the bank contracts with users, controls transfers
      and settlement, records balances, and owes repayment to users.

      - It could be a targeted solution for a state where Mosaic lacks authority.


      **How it hinders**


      - Bank custody alone does not settle Mosaic’s licensing position. Mosaic could
      still need authority if it controls payment activity or has the main obligation
      to users.

      - It requires bank agreements, customer terms, new funds flows, system changes,
      and operating procedures.

      - This extra work could delay the migration.

      - Mosaic cannot measure the legal benefit until the parties define their roles.


      This is a possible licensing solution. It is not a complete compliance solution.


      ### Issues that remain under both


      1. **Open alerts and SAR history.** The parties must review the alerts and suspicious
      activity report history. Bank custody does not resolve past problems or assign
      unfinished cases.


      2. **Identity records.** Mosaic must decide whether its compliance program permits
      use of Harbor’s earlier identity results. It also needs a plan for missing underlying
      records.


      3. **Monitoring continuity.** Harbor’s monitoring contract ends at close. Mosaic
      must test the new transaction feed and prevent a monitoring gap.


      4. **State authority.** Both structures need state-by-state review. The bank
      structure changes the analysis but does not remove it.


      5. **Division of duties.** The agreements must allocate identity checks, sanctions
      screening, monitoring, alert review, regulatory filings, privacy duties, and
      customer support.


      6. **Customer migration and data use.** Both approaches require review of data
      transfer rights, changed customer terms, and access to Mosaic’s products.


      7. **Welcome bonus.** The offer terms, eligibility rules, disclosures, and funds
      flow need review under either structure.


      **Working recommendation**


      Keep the **Current approach** as the planning baseline. Use firm launch gates
      for state authority, identity records, alert review, and monitoring readiness.


      Keep the **Bank-held funds structure** as a fallback or targeted state solution.
      Use it only if a detailed funds-flow and responsibility map shows a clear benefit
      that is greater than the added cost and delay risk.


      If a gate is not met, narrow the migration. Delay affected markets or user groups
      instead of delaying the entire acquisition.


      The bank structure remains hypothetical. This comparison does not change the
      current direction.


      **No external authority retrieved.** The state licensing and federal financial-crime
      conclusions remain provisional.'
    record_revision: 2221b3cc29365d5b2609f36cad5c9dc70420cb332e6a7d37355c0d742caeff30
    source_version: 2221b3cc29365d5b2609f36cad5c9dc70420cb332e6a7d37355c0d742caeff30

    captured_record:
      message_id: MSG-20260911-c4625a
      content: 'The **Current approach** best supports the planned day-one migration.
        The **Bank-held funds structure** could help in selected states, but its legal
        benefit is not yet clear. It also adds work that could delay the migration.


        **Objective**


        Complete the acquisition and migration on schedule. Avoid operating without
        required state authority. Prevent gaps in identity checks, transaction monitoring,
        and alert handling.


        ### Current approach


        Mosaic becomes the operating provider after the asset purchase.


        **How it helps**


        - It preserves the current product and migration plan.

        - It avoids new bank agreements, customer terms, and system links.

        - It gives Mosaic the better chance of meeting the day-one target.


        **How it hinders**


        - Mosaic plans to use or assume Harbor’s licenses in four new markets. Their
        continued use after an asset purchase is not confirmed.

        - Mosaic directly takes on the unresolved identity-record, monitoring, and
        alert issues.

        - A single large migration increases the effect of a failure.


        This approach needs firm launch gates. Mosaic should exclude a state or user
        group if the required review is not complete.


        ### Bank-held funds structure


        A partner bank holds customer funds. The other operating roles remain undefined.


        **How it helps**


        - It could reduce Mosaic’s state money-transmission obligations.

        - The benefit is more likely if the bank contracts with users, controls transfers
        and settlement, records balances, and owes repayment to users.

        - It could be a targeted solution for a state where Mosaic lacks authority.


        **How it hinders**


        - Bank custody alone does not settle Mosaic’s licensing position. Mosaic could
        still need authority if it controls payment activity or has the main obligation
        to users.

        - It requires bank agreements, customer terms, new funds flows, system changes,
        and operating procedures.

        - This extra work could delay the migration.

        - Mosaic cannot measure the legal benefit until the parties define their roles.


        This is a possible licensing solution. It is not a complete compliance solution.


        ### Issues that remain under both


        1. **Open alerts and SAR history.** The parties must review the alerts and
        suspicious activity report history. Bank custody does not resolve past problems
        or assign unfinished cases.


        2. **Identity records.** Mosaic must decide whether its compliance program
        permits use of Harbor’s earlier identity results. It also needs a plan for
        missing underlying records.


        3. **Monitoring continuity.** Harbor’s monitoring contract ends at close.
        Mosaic must test the new transaction feed and prevent a monitoring gap.


        4. **State authority.** Both structures need state-by-state review. The bank
        structure changes the analysis but does not remove it.


        5. **Division of duties.** The agreements must allocate identity checks, sanctions
        screening, monitoring, alert review, regulatory filings, privacy duties, and
        customer support.


        6. **Customer migration and data use.** Both approaches require review of
        data transfer rights, changed customer terms, and access to Mosaic’s products.


        7. **Welcome bonus.** The offer terms, eligibility rules, disclosures, and
        funds flow need review under either structure.


        **Working recommendation**


        Keep the **Current approach** as the planning baseline. Use firm launch gates
        for state authority, identity records, alert review, and monitoring readiness.


        Keep the **Bank-held funds structure** as a fallback or targeted state solution.
        Use it only if a detailed funds-flow and responsibility map shows a clear
        benefit that is greater than the added cost and delay risk.


        If a gate is not met, narrow the migration. Delay affected markets or user
        groups instead of delaying the entire acquisition.


        The bank structure remains hypothetical. This comparison does not change the
        current direction.


        **No external authority retrieved.** The state licensing and federal financial-crime
        conclusions remain provisional.'

    path: 03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/conversations/CONV-20260909-5299cf.md
    available_excerpt: 'The **Current approach** better supports the planned day-one
      migration. The **Bank-held funds structure** could help in selected states,
      but its legal benefit depends on the bank’s full role. It also adds work that
      could delay migration.

      **Objective**

      Complete the acquisition and migration on schedule. Do not operate without required
      state authority. Prevent gaps in identity checks, transaction monitoring, and
      alert handling.

      ### Current approach

      Mosaic becomes the operating provider after the asset purchase.

      **Benefits**

      - Keeps the current product and migration design.

      - Avoids new bank agreements, funds flows, customer terms, and system links.

      - Gives Mosaic the better chance of meeting the day-one target.

      **Trade-offs and added risks**

      - Mosaic plans to use or assume Harbor’s licenses in four new markets. Their
      continued use after an asset purchase is not confirmed.

      - Mosaic would directly manage the migrated accounts and related compliance
      controls.

      - Moving all users at once increases the effect of a control failure.

      These risks arise from, or become worse under, the current approach. They are
      separate from Harbor’s existing compliance problems.

      **Issues that remain**

      - Whether Mosaic can rely on Harbor’s prior identity checks.

      - How Mosaic will address missing identity records.

      - What Harbor’s open alerts and suspicious activity report history show.

      - Whether the new monitoring feed can operate without a gap.

      - Whether the migration communications and welcome bonus meet applicable requirements.

      ### Bank-held funds structure

      A partner bank holds customer funds. The other operating roles are not yet defined.

      **Benefits**

      - It could reduce Mosaic’s licensing burden in selected states.

      - The benefit is stronger if the bank contracts with users, controls transfers
      and settlement, records balances, and owes repayment to users.

      - It could provide a targeted solution where Mosaic cannot obtain authority
      before launch.

      **Trade-offs and added risks**

      - Bank custody alone does not settle Mosaic’s licensing position. Mosaic could
      still need authority based on the activities it performs.

      - This structure needs new agreements, funds flows, customer terms, technical
      changes, and operating procedures.

      - These changes could delay migration.

      - Unclear duties could create control gaps or duplicate work between Mosaic
      and the bank.

      These are new execution and responsibility risks created by the bank structure.

      **Issues that remain**

      - Harbor’s open alerts and past suspicious activity reports still require review.

      - Mosaic still needs a valid basis for relying on Harbor’s identity checks.

      - Monitoring must still be tested before Harbor’s vendor contract ends.

      - State-by-state review remains necessary. The structure changes the analysis;
      it does not remove it.

      - Data-transfer rights, privacy duties, customer terms, product access, and
      the welcome bonus remain open.

      ### Shared issues

      Both approaches require:

      1. A state-by-state review of each party’s activities and authority.

      2. Review of the open alerts and suspicious activity report history.

      3. A documented plan for identity-check reliance and missing records.

      4. Tested monitoring before Harbor’s vendor contract ends.

      5. Clear allocation of identity, sanctions, monitoring, filing, privacy, and
      customer-support duties.

      6. Review of migration terms, data use, product access, and the welcome bonus.

      **Working recommendation**

      Keep the **Current approach** as the planning baseline because it better supports
      the timing. Apply firm launch gates for state authority, alert review, identity
      records, and monitoring readiness.

      Keep the **Bank-held funds structure** as a targeted fallback. Use it only if
      a detailed funds-flow and responsibility map shows a clear licensing benefit
      that is greater than the added cost and delay risk.

      If a gate is not met, delay the affected states or user groups rather than the
      whole acquisition. The bank structure remains hypothetical and does not change
      the current direction.

      **No external authority retrieved.** The state licensing and federal financial-crime
      conclusions remain provisional.
