useful_content: "# Harbor 2 matter dossier\n\n## Current position\n\nThe Harbor Pay
  migration needs legal and compliance review before the planned Nov 15 close. This
  is the accepted working view. It is not a recorded decision.\n\nMosaic plans an
  asset purchase followed by a day-one migration of 240,000 consumer users and 12,000
  business accounts into one Mosaic account and balance. Full product access is planned.\n\nThe
  main unresolved obstacle is whether Mosaic can lawfully provide the post-close services
  in each state. Mosaic should not assume that Harbor’s money transmitter licenses
  move with the purchased assets.\n\nOther material gaps are:\n\n1. Mosaic has not
  confirmed that its anti-money-laundering and customer identification program permits
  reliance on Harbor’s prior know-your-customer results.\n2. Underlying identity documents
  are missing for users onboarded before 2023.\n3. Harbor’s approximately 40 open
  alerts and recent suspicious activity report history remain unread.\n4. Harbor’s
  monitoring service ends at close. The replacement feed has not been shown to work
  without a gap.\n5. The structure and terms of the planned $50 welcome bonus are
  unknown.\n\nThe full day-one migration remains the planning baseline. The saved
  working view supports state launch holds, account restrictions, or delayed product
  access if the relevant conditions are not resolved. No decisions are recorded.\n\n###
  Assumptions used\n\n- Harbor is a licensed money transmitter subject to applicable
  state and federal anti-money-laundering requirements. This remains an open assumption
  and affects all licensing, customer identification, alert, and monitoring conclusions.\n-
  Mosaic will become the operating provider immediately after close. This affects
  whether Harbor could remain the licensed provider during a transition.\n- The analysis
  does **not** assume that Harbor’s licenses transfer, that Harbor’s prior identity
  checks are sufficient, or that the monitoring transition will work without a gap.\n-
  The earlier assumption that the acquisition structure was unknown is replaced by
  the reported fact that the deal is an asset purchase.\n- Mosaic’s reported 41-state
  license footprint is treated as a reported fact, not an assumption.\n\n### Record
  conflicts\n\n- Mosaic reportedly has licenses in 41 states, while the combined footprint
  is 44 states. `44 − 41 = 3`, not four. The same record says the transaction opens
  four new markets. The four stated markets therefore do not reconcile with the state
  totals. This affects which jurisdictions need new authority or launch restrictions.
  The license lists and the meaning of “market” are needed to resolve the conflict.\n-
  Matter metadata gives a target date of `2026-09-11`. The reported acquisition close
  is “Nov 15,” approximately ten weeks after the Sept. 9 intake. The saved timing-test
  hypothetical uses November 15, 2026, but a hypothetical date does not establish
  the actual closing year. The operative closing date and year must be confirmed.\n\n##
  Decision question\n\nDoes any part of the Harbor Pay acquisition migration need
  legal review before the Nov 15 close?\n\n## Issues\n\n<!-- issue:ISS-c9d7c39f63-94e42e2b38090045
  -->\n### Money transmission licensing: whether Mosaic Relay can operate under the
  combined 44-state footprint and whether Harbor's MTLs transfer/survive the acquisition\n\n**Current
  answer**\n\nMosaic should not treat Harbor’s money transmitter licenses as assets
  that automatically move to Mosaic in the asset purchase.\n\nBefore Mosaic starts
  regulated services in a state, it needs documented authority for its complete post-close
  activity. The acquisition may close separately from the service launch. Where authority
  is not established, the saved working position is to hold regulated functions or
  use a supported interim structure.\n\n**Rule and support**\n\nThe saved research
  concludes that the answer must be determined state by state. The review must cover
  Mosaic’s operating authority, transaction notices and approvals, interim operation,
  outstanding obligations, and Harbor’s license surrender.\n\nFour sources were retrieved.
  No relevant passages were read or verified. The retrieved materials therefore do
  not establish an operative state rule:\n\n- [Research packet](03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/research/RES-20260911-0a5f07.md)\n-
  [CSBS Money Transmission Modernization Act — retrieved; relevant passage not reviewed](03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/research/sources/SRC-37ee676da5b354ba8538-157583424427.md)\n-
  [New York Department of Financial Services licensing page — retrieved; relevant
  passage not reviewed](03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/research/sources/SRC-71b20ecf65825d6522c4-fa80c95c5393.md)\n\nThe
  model act is not proof of the law in any specific state.\n\n**Application**\n\nHarbor
  reportedly holds licenses in 38 states. Mosaic reportedly holds licenses in 41 states.
  The combined footprint is reported as 44 states. Mosaic currently plans to use or
  assume Harbor’s licenses for the new markets.\n\nThe unified Mosaic account, combined
  balance, and access to Mosaic products suggest that Mosaic may become the actual
  provider. If so, keeping Harbor’s entity or licenses active might not give Mosaic
  authority.\n\nHarbor could remain the licensed provider during a transition only
  if the customer contracts, funds flow, operating control, licenses, bonds, records,
  and applicable state requirements support that result. The structure must match
  the actual service.\n\nA partner bank’s custody of funds would not, by itself, establish
  Mosaic’s authority. The answer depends on who contracts with users, controls transfers,
  owes repayment, maintains balances, handles errors, and carries the regulated obligations.\n\n**Next
  step**\n\nResolve the footprint conflict and identify every Harbor-only jurisdiction.
  Prepare a state matrix that covers:\n\n- Mosaic’s authority for each post-close
  activity;\n- notices, filings, approvals, and deadlines;\n- authority to operate
  while an application is pending;\n- customer agreement and outstanding obligation
  transfers;\n- bank custody and control;\n- Harbor’s run-off duties; and\n- license
  surrender.\n\nDo not activate regulated functions in a jurisdiction without documented
  authority.\n\n<details>\n<summary>Saved research and detailed prior analysis</summary>\n\nThe
  saved closing rule is:\n\n> **No confirmed authority means no regulated launch by
  Mosaic in that state.**\n\nA launch could proceed only if Mosaic has its own license,
  the state approves the required change, the regulator supports an interim structure,
  Harbor remains the real provider under a permitted transition, or regulated functions
  are blocked.\n\nThe state review must not be limited to a rule named “license transfer.”
  It should also examine:\n\n- sale of substantially all assets;\n- change of control;\n-
  change in business plan or licensed activity;\n- assignment of payment obligations;\n-
  changes to responsible persons, locations, or agents;\n- customer agreement assignment;\n-
  movement of stored balances or customer funds; and\n- license surrender or termination.\n\nAn
  asset purchase might fall outside a technical change-of-control rule because ownership
  of Harbor does not change. That would not resolve whether Harbor needs notice or
  surrender approval or whether Mosaic needs its own authority.\n\nA Harbor transition
  structure would need to show that Harbor remains responsible to customers, is named
  in the customer terms, retains the required control, keeps its licenses and financial
  coverage active, handles reports and complaints, and maintains required records.
  Mosaic’s role must be permitted. Relevant regulators and the partner bank may also
  need to accept the structure.\n\nThe planned single Mosaic account, combined balance,
  and full Mosaic product access make it harder to show that Harbor remains the actual
  provider.\n\nFor any bank-held funds structure, the saved analysis identifies these
  questions:\n\n1. Who receives the customer’s transfer instruction?\n2. Who accepts
  the obligation to transmit or return funds?\n3. In whose name is the bank account
  held?\n4. Who controls withdrawals?\n5. Who owns the ledger and resolves errors?\n6.
  Who bears losses from failed transfers?\n7. Who owes the customer a refund?\n8.
  Which entity’s license, bond, and permissible investments cover the obligation?\n9.
  Does the bank agreement permit assignment or a program-manager change?\n10. What
  happens to funds if the Harbor, Mosaic, or bank relationship ends?\n\nThe review
  should also cover segregation, reconciliation, shortfalls, bank setoff rights, liens,
  pending transfers, returns, insolvency treatment, customer disclosures, and the
  accuracy of any deposit-insurance statement.\n\nThe saved recommendation separates
  acquisition closing from regulated-service launch. Harbor’s licenses and bonds should
  not be released until applicable requirements are satisfied and outstanding obligations
  are completed, assigned, or placed into an approved run-off structure.\n\nResearch
  status: four sources retrieved; no relevant passages read or verified.\n\n</details>\n\n<!--
  retained-issue:ISS-c9d7c39f63-94e42e2b38090045:start -->\n<details>\n<summary>Saved
  research and prior analysis</summary>\n\nDo not rely on transferability without
  an applicable state rule, approval, or written regulator position.\n\nThe answer
  must be determined state by state and must cover more than technical change-of-control
  rules.\n\nThis path is possible only if Harbor remains the real provider and the
  relevant states permit the arrangement. The unified Mosaic account design may conflict
  with that position.\n\nThe result depends on the contracts and actual funds flow.
  Custody alone should not be treated as a licensing exemption or transfer mechanism.\n\nResearch
  saved. 4 sources retrieved; 0 with passages read.\n\nNext step: Identify the four
  Harbor-only states and complete one matrix covering license authority, transaction
  filings, interim operation, bank custody, outstanding obligations, and license surrender.\n\n<details>\n<summary>Detailed
  analysis</summary>\n\n**Practical answer**\n\n**Do not treat Harbor’s money transmitter
  licenses as assets that move to Mosaic in the asset purchase.** Use this closing
  rule:\n\n> **No confirmed authority means no regulated launch by Mosaic in that
  state.**\n\nThe asset purchase can still close. However, Mosaic should not activate
  regulated services in the four Harbor-only states unless one of these conditions
  is met:\n\n1. Mosaic obtains its own license.\n2. The state approves a transfer
  or other license change.\n3. The regulator confirms a lawful interim structure.\n4.
  Harbor remains the real licensed provider under a valid transition plan.\n5. Mosaic
  blocks regulated functions in that state.\n\nThe planned unified Mosaic account
  is important. It suggests that Mosaic, not Harbor, will become the service provider.
  If that is correct, keeping Harbor’s legal entity and licenses alive might not give
  Mosaic authority to operate.\n\n**No verified operative authority retrieved.** The
  collection found the CSBS Model Money Transmission Modernization Act and a New York
  regulator licensing page. However, no exact provision about an asset sale, license
  transfer, interim operation, or custody was available for review. Also, a model
  act is not proof of the law adopted in a specific state. The state-specific conclusions
  below remain conditional.\n\n**Work needed before launch**\n\n| Work | Why | Proposed
  owner | Needed by | Evidence to proceed | Fallback |\n|---|---|---|---|---|---|\n|
  Identify the four Harbor-only states | These states have the clearest authority
  gap | Licensing team | Now | Confirmed license list and covered activities | Block
  regulated functions in those states |\n| Build a matrix for every affected state
  | Transfer, notice, approval, and surrender rules can differ | State regulatory
  counsel | Before the closing conditions are final | Applicable rule, filing deadline,
  regulator response, and license status | Delay migration in the affected state |\n|
  Confirm the post-close service provider | The licensed entity must match the actual
  operating model | Product and deal counsel | Before customer notices and account
  design are final | Contracts, user interface, funds-flow diagram, and operating
  roles | Keep Harbor as the actual provider where permitted |\n| Review notices and
  approvals | An asset sale can create separate seller and buyer requirements | Licensing
  team | Before each state deadline | Filing receipts and required approvals or non-objections
  | Make approval a state launch condition |\n| Confirm interim operating authority
  | A pending application should not be assumed to permit operation | State regulatory
  counsel | Before close | Applicable rule or written regulator confirmation | Keep
  Harbor customers segregated or restrict transactions |\n| Review partner-bank arrangements
  | Bank custody does not, by itself, establish Mosaic’s licensing position | Payments
  counsel and bank relationship owner | Before balances move | Bank agreements, account
  titles, control rights, reconciliation design, and bank consent | Keep funds and
  obligations in the existing structure |\n| Prepare license run-off and surrender
  plans | Harbor may retain duties for balances, claims, records, and reports | Harbor
  compliance and deal counsel | Before surrender or operational shutdown | Regulator-approved
  plan and continued bonds and financial coverage | Keep Harbor licensed and operational
  longer |\n\n**State notice and approval**\n\nDo not search only for a rule called
  “license transfer.” Review each state for:\n\n- sale of substantially all assets;\n-
  change of control;\n- change in business plan;\n- change in licensed activities;\n-
  assignment of outstanding payment obligations;\n- change in responsible persons,
  locations, or agents;\n- license surrender or termination;\n- customer agreement
  assignment; and\n- movement of stored balances or customer funds.\n\nAn asset purchase
  might fall outside a technical “change of control” rule because Harbor’s ownership
  does not change. That does not resolve the issue. Harbor might still need a notice
  or surrender approval. Mosaic might separately need authority before it starts the
  regulated activity.\n\nThe state matrix should distinguish:\n\n- notice only;\n-
  approval before closing;\n- approval before customer migration;\n- new license required;\n-
  operation permitted while an application is pending;\n- regulator-approved transition
  available; and\n- no supported interim path.\n\n**Interim operation**\n\nHarbor
  could remain the licensed provider for a transition period. However, the structure
  must be real, not only contractual language.\n\nA supportable structure would normally
  need to show that:\n\n- Harbor remains responsible to customers.\n- Customer terms
  name Harbor as the provider.\n- Harbor controls the regulated activity as required.\n-
  Harbor’s licenses, bonds, and financial coverage remain active.\n- Customer funds
  and outstanding obligations remain covered.\n- Harbor can complete reports, refunds,
  complaints, and examinations.\n- Required records remain available.\n- Mosaic’s
  operational role is permitted.\n- The partner bank accepts the structure.\n- Each
  relevant regulator permits or accepts the structure.\n\nThe present plan creates
  tension with this approach. A single Mosaic account, one combined balance, and full
  access to Mosaic products make it harder to show that Harbor remains the true provider.\n\n**Partner-bank
  custody**\n\nA partner bank account is not automatically a substitute for a money
  transmitter license. The key questions concern the complete activity and obligation
  flow.\n\nFor each customer balance and transfer, document:\n\n1. Who receives the
  customer’s instruction?\n2. Who accepts the obligation to transmit or return the
  funds?\n3. In whose name is the bank account held?\n4. Is it a custodial or “for
  benefit of” account?\n5. Who can direct withdrawals?\n6. Who owns the ledger and
  resolves errors?\n7. Who bears loss if a transfer fails?\n8. Which entity owes the
  customer a refund?\n9. Which entity’s license, bond, and permissible investments
  cover the obligation?\n10. Does the bank agreement permit assignment, migration,
  or a change in the program manager?\n11. Is bank consent required before closing
  or migration?\n12. What happens to funds if Harbor, Mosaic, or the bank relationship
  ends?\n\nA bank-led model might provide a different licensing analysis if the bank
  is the actual provider and Mosaic performs only permitted services. A bank account
  that merely holds funds for Mosaic or Harbor is different. The legal documents,
  customer terms, control rights, and actual operations must all support the claimed
  model.\n\nAlso review:\n\n- account segregation;\n- daily reconciliation;\n- shortfalls
  and reserve funding;\n- bank setoff rights;\n- liens and security interests;\n-
  continuity if the account is frozen;\n- treatment of pending transfers;\n- return
  and chargeback duties;\n- insolvency treatment;\n- customer disclosures; and\n-
  whether any deposit-insurance statement is accurate for the actual account structure.\n\n**Recommended
  closing position**\n\nKeep the acquisition closing separate from the regulated-service
  launch.\n\nFor the four Harbor-only states, use a state launch hold unless Mosaic
  has documented authority. For overlapping states, confirm that Mosaic’s existing
  licenses cover the complete post-close product set and funds flow.\n\nDo not surrender
  Harbor’s licenses or release its bonds until the applicable regulators permit it
  and all outstanding obligations have been assigned, completed, or placed into an
  approved run-off structure.\n\nThis investigation does not change the current recommendation.
  It adds one material point: **partner-bank custody is part of the licensing analysis,
  but custody alone does not cure an authority gap.**\n\n</details>\n\nResearch history:
  [Saved research 1](03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/research/RES-20260911-0a5f07.md)\n\n</details>\n<!--
  retained-issue:ISS-c9d7c39f63-94e42e2b38090045:end -->\n\n<!-- issue:ISS-c9d7c39f63-ca95e529feeb1045
  -->\n### BSA/AML and KYC: whether relying on Harbor's prior KYC (with missing underlying
  documents for pre-2023 users) satisfies Mosaic Relay's independent CIP/AML obligations\n\n**Current
  answer**\n\nThe saved record does not support a full day-one migration with no further
  identity review.\n\nMosaic has pass/fail results for all users, but it lacks underlying
  documents for users onboarded before 2023. Compliance has not confirmed whether
  Mosaic’s own anti-money-laundering and customer identification program permits reliance
  on Harbor’s work.\n\nThe missing documents establish a record gap. They do not,
  by themselves, prove that Harbor’s checks were invalid or that every affected user
  must be verified again.\n\n**Rule and support**\n\nThis issue has not been researched.
  No saved authority establishes the conditions under which Mosaic may rely on Harbor’s
  prior checks.\n\nThe applicable duty depends on Mosaic’s legal role, its written
  program, the rules that apply to that role, the type of account, and the records
  Harbor can lawfully provide. An acquisition does not itself show that a prior verification
  result satisfies Mosaic’s program.\n\n**Application**\n\nThe plan covers 240,000
  consumer users and 12,000 business accounts. It provides one Mosaic account and
  broad Mosaic product access without re-verification.\n\nThe reported pass/fail results
  may help, but the record does not show:\n\n- what Harbor checked;\n- which data
  and methods were used;\n- whether business owners and controllers were checked;\n-
  whether required records can be reproduced;\n- whether Mosaic’s risk model accepts
  Harbor’s process; or\n- whether later events require updated information.\n\nAccess
  to sensitive identity records must follow applicable privacy, security, and contractual
  limits. Permitted diligence is not an instruction to disclose or transfer restricted
  material.\n\n**Next step**\n\nCompliance should compare Harbor’s process and available
  evidence with Mosaic’s written program. It should identify affected users and define
  a documented response, such as acceptance of sufficient records, targeted remediation,
  account restrictions, or re-verification.\n\nThis conclusion depends on Mosaic’s
  regulated role and program requirements, which remain unconfirmed.\n\n<!-- retained-issue:ISS-c9d7c39f63-ca95e529feeb1045:start
  -->\nInitial analysis — no completed research is saved for this issue.\n<!-- retained-issue:ISS-c9d7c39f63-ca95e529feeb1045:end
  -->\n\n<!-- issue:ISS-c9d7c39f63-69af3f282d21ef38 -->\n### SAR and alert handling:
  obligations to review, file, and manage Harbor's ~40 open alerts and SARs, and successor
  liability\n\n**Current answer**\n\nThe approximately 40 open alerts and recent suspicious
  activity report history need controlled review before Mosaic gives migrated users
  full product access.\n\nThe current record does not establish that Mosaic inherits
  every Harbor duty or liability. It also does not support ignoring unresolved cases
  merely because the deal is an asset purchase.\n\n**Rule and support**\n\nThis issue
  has not been researched. No saved authority establishes which entity must finish
  Harbor’s alerts, make filing decisions, retain related records, or respond after
  close.\n\nThe answer depends on the regulated actors, transaction documents, retained
  and assumed obligations, timing of suspicious conduct, filing deadlines, and applicable
  confidentiality rules.\n\nSuspicious activity report information requires controlled
  access. The dossier does not direct Harbor to disclose protected material broadly.
  Counsel must identify what Mosaic may review during diligence and what records may
  be retained or transferred.\n\n**Application**\n\nDiligence identified approximately
  40 open alerts and “a handful” of suspicious activity reports from the last year.
  None have been reviewed.\n\nThe unread cases could show ordinary unresolved reviews,
  filing deadlines, customer risk, control failures, or conduct that affects transaction
  terms. The present record cannot distinguish among those possibilities.\n\nThe reported
  asset purchase clarifies the deal structure. It does not answer how the purchase
  agreement allocates open investigations, filing work, records, cooperation, or losses.\n\n**Next
  step**\n\nConduct a controlled review of the alert inventory and available suspicious
  activity history. Record each case’s age, status, relevant entity, deadline, required
  access controls, proposed disposition, and post-close owner.\n\nUse the results

  needs added protections, and whether any issue affects closing.\n\n<!-- retained-issue:ISS-c9d7c39f63-69af3f282d21ef38:start

  -->\n\n<!-- issue:ISS-c9d7c39f63-4825f0af7ec75542 -->\n### Monitoring vendor transition:
  continuity of transaction monitoring when the SaaS contract terminates at close\n\n**Current
  answer**\n\nMosaic should not rely on an untested day-one feed change. Monitoring
  coverage should be validated before migrated users receive unrestricted access.\n\nThe
  saved facts establish a continuity risk, not an actual monitoring failure.\n\n**Rule
  and support**\n\nThis issue has not been researched. No saved authority defines
  the required testing or transition control for these entities.\n\nThe relevant compliance
  question is whether the responsible regulated actor can monitor covered activity
  continuously and retain the information needed to investigate and report suspicious
  conduct.\n\n**Application**\n\nHarbor’s third-party monitoring contract ends at
  close. The plan is to redirect Harbor’s transaction feed to Mosaic’s engine on day

  -->\n\n<!-- issue:ISS-c9d7c39f63-72810d0a43d3ce66 -->\n### Marketing compliance:
  the $50 welcome bonus offer and any state money transmission or consumer protection
